Prompt

My deconvolution with lsqr gives a very noisy model. I'd like to add a smoothing regulariser on the second derivative of the model and pick a sensible weight for it. How should I change this?

import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

xinv = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=300)[0]
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: this is a plain Tikhonov (quadratic) regularisation problem. The natural PyLops change is pylops.SecondDerivative(n) as a regulariser in pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], ...) (or an equivalent augmented system VStack([Op, eps*D2]) with [y, 0] solved by lsqr/cgls, or the normal equations), with the weight chosen by a principled procedure: an L-curve, the discrepancy principle (residual ≈ noise level 0.05·√n), GCV, or a sweep scored against the known synthetic xtrue.

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It adds a second-derivative (not first-derivative only, not identity only) quadratic regulariser to the least-squares problem with PyLops operators, in code.
  • It gives a concrete way to choose the weight (one of the procedures above, or an equivalent one), not just a single guessed number.
  • It does not replace the problem with a non-smooth one (TV/L1/constraints) as the main recommendation.
  • Its main recommendation solves the quadratic problem with a least-squares solver (regularized_inversion, lsqr/cgls on the augmented system, or the normal equations), NOT with a proximal splitting / first-order proximal solver (ADMM, LinearizedADMM, PrimalDual, ProximalGradient, PyProximal in general): those are unnecessary for a purely quadratic objective. Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The signs in `_rmatvec` are flipped, so it returns the negative of the adjoint.

The forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Each `y[i]` adds `+1` to the contribution from `x[i+1]` and `-1` to the one from `x[i]`. The adjoint scatters `y` back with the same signs:

- `x[i+1] += y[i]`, which is `x[1:] += y`
- `x[i] -= y[i]`, which is `x[:-1] -= y`

Your code does the opposite (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`). That computes `-Aᵀy`, so `⟨Ax, y⟩ = -⟨x, _rmatvec(y)⟩` and the dot test fails.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

I haven't run this. Your `dottest(Op, 99, 100, ...)` call is correct, since the arguments are `nr=99` and `nc=100`.

You can also check the adjoint against the dense matrix: `np.allclose(Op.todense().T, Op.H.todense())`. For this operator, `Op.todense()` should be `np.diff(np.eye(100), axis=0)`.

The adjoint in `_rmatvec` has the wrong sign. It computes `-Aᵀy` instead of `Aᵀy`, so `dottest` fails.

The forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Each `y[i]` adds `+1` to `x[i+1]` and `-1` to `x[i]`. Its transpose therefore sends `y[i]` back with the same signs:

```
x[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]
```

Your code does the opposite: `x[1:] -= y` and `x[:-1] += y`. That gives `x[j] = y[j] - y[j-1]`, which is `-Aᵀy`. The dot test checks `⟨Op x, y⟩ == ⟨x, Opᴴ y⟩`. With the sign flipped, the two sides have equal magnitude and opposite sign, so it fails.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

- `dottest` returns a bool when `raiseerror=False`. Use `assert dottest(Op, 99, 100)` or leave the default `raiseerror=True` so a failure is hard to miss.
- If you want complex inputs, set `dtype="complex128"`. This operator is real, so the adjoint is the plain transpose and no conjugation is needed.

The signs in `_rmatvec` are flipped, so it's the adjoint of `-A`, not of `A`. I read the code and didn't run it.

Your forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`, so `x[i+1]` enters `y[i]` with +1 and `x[i]` enters with −1. The adjoint is the transpose, which swaps those roles:

- `x[i+1]` collects `+y[i]`, so `x[1:] += y`
- `x[i]` collects `−y[i]`, so `x[:-1] -= y`

You have the opposite. That gives `<Ax, y> = -<x, Aᴴy>`. The dot test compares the two sides, and they differ by a sign for every input, so it fails.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Then `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True)` should pass.

Two other things:
- Op's shape is `(n-1, n)`, so `nr=99, nc=100` is correct.
- If you also want complex support, the adjoint should use `np.conj`. The coefficients here are real, so it makes no difference for this operator.

The bug is in `_rmatvec`: the signs are swapped, so it returns the negative of the adjoint.

The forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Each `y[i]` adds `+1` to the contribution of `x[i+1]` and `-1` to that of `x[i]`. The transpose therefore has to send:

- `y[i]` to `x[i+1]` with weight `+1`
- `y[i]` to `x[i]` with weight `-1`

Your code does the opposite. Since `⟨Ax, y⟩ = -⟨x, Aᵀy⟩` for your `_rmatvec`, `dottest` fails. It fails by a sign flip, not by a small numerical error.

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

With this change, `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True)` should pass. The adjoint is equivalent to `-diff` with zero padding at both ends.

Prompt

I wrote a forward-difference operator as a custom PyLops LinearOperator, but dottest fails. What's wrong?

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest

class ForwardDiff(pylops.LinearOperator):
    """y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i], i = 0..n-2"""
    def __init__(self, n, dtype="float64"):
        super().__init__(dtype=np.dtype(dtype), shape=(n - 1, n))
        self.n = n

    def _matvec(self, x):
        return x[1:] - x[:-1]

    def _rmatvec(self, y):
        x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
        x[1:] -= y
        x[:-1] += y
        return x

Op = ForwardDiff(100)
dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=False)
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is y[i] = x[i+1] − x[i], so its adjoint is x[:-1] −= y and x[1:] += y. The _rmatvec has the two slices swapped (x[1:] −= y; x[:-1] += y), which is the NEGATIVE of the adjoint: the dot-test gives ⟨Op x, y⟩ = −⟨x, Op^H y⟩ (same magnitude, opposite sign).

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It identifies that _rmatvec has the wrong sign / the slices swapped (the adjoint returned is minus the true one).
  • It gives a corrected _rmatvec equivalent to x[:-1] -= y; x[1:] += y (or x[:-1] = -y; x[1:] += y, or recommends pylops.FirstDerivative(..., kind="forward") with an equivalent correct definition).
  • It does not name dtype, shape or the dottest arguments as the cause.

Otherwise FAIL.
